\subsection*{Exemplary Results for Solvent Substitution}
To apply the above-developed pipeline for material discovery, we predict solubility parameters on a database of 5000 VOCs\cite{CompsolDatabaseRevised2024}. The final prediction is an average of the predictions of every individual model of the n-fold validation.
% While we cannot verify the correctness of our pipeline on unseen data, we employed cross-validation and transfer learning from large foundational models to increase the chances of good generalization.
While we cannot directly verify the correctness of our pipeline on unseen data, the use of transfer learning from large foundational models increases the chances of good generalization. Additionally,  a rigorous cross validation approach gives a strong indication of our model's generalization abilities.


We further applied out solvent substitution tool on common reference solvents of concern to verify the validity of the predictions on a case by case basis. In \cref{fig:predictions}, we depict the reference solvents along with the solvents of closest distance without screening and the solvent of closest distance where H300 and H400 symbols were eliminated by screening.  Overall, the predictions are sensible given that functional groups of reference solvents are often represented as well in the substitution alternatives.
% \textcolor{blue}{The value of our pipeline comes in discarding molecules that contain the functional groups, yet are unsuitable for the solubility task. }
This is to be expected because chemical interactions encoded in the solubility parameters are highly correlated to molecular structure. However, the predictions of solubility parameters go beyond simple structure analyses because they compare directly energy interaction properties excluding chemicals that have very similar structures, but distinct solubility behavior. We discuss the results on a case basis below,



\begin{itemize}
    \item Dichloromethane is proposed to be substituted by 2-Bromobut-2-ene. While this is still a halogenated chemical that is not considered ``green'' in a strict definition it passes the rudimentary screening applied herein. It is a sensible suggestion given the highly halogenated Dichloromethane.  However, its functionality as a solvent is questionable due to its reactivity\cite{orkinPhotochemistryBromineContainingFluorinated2002}.  The next closest candidate is food agent  2,5-Dimethylthiophene just as suggested for substitution of Chlorobenzene.
    \item The proposed substitutions of Chlorobenzene and Toluene are 2,5-Dimethylthiophene and 1,4-Dihydronaphthalene, where the functionality of the aromatic rings in the original solvents are imitated. We have not found any instance of them being used as a solvent before.
    \item Methanol is proposed to be replaced by 1,3-butanediol, which has been employed as a solvent before\cite{romeroFeasibility13butanediolSolvent2010}. However, the comparatively larger distance suggests that solubility might not always be similar.
    \item N,N-Dimethylformamide is proposed to be substituted by Gamma-Valerolactone (GVL), which is perfectly consistent with green solvent fabrication of perovksite solar cells\cite{hanGreenSolutionProcessing2024,kimEcoFriendlyAllLayerGreen2024,baeAdvancingPerovskiteSolar2025}.
    \item Ethylene carbonate is proposed to be substituted by dimethylsulfone which has been demonstrated successfully as a battery electrolyte before\cite{hofmannNovelElectrolyteMixtures2015}. The prediction is therefore consistent with literature.
    \item Some more exotic substitution suggestions by our tool are to use the flavor enhancers Methyl isovalerate and 1-Methyl-2,3-cyclohexadione (and 2,5-Dimethylthiophene) as solvents. While we could not find any instance of these ideas yet and economical feasibility is questionable, they demonstrate that out tool may suggest unconventional new solvent alternatives,which is entirely in line with the notion of material discovery.
\end{itemize}

We sum up that our tool has high likelihood of leading to new data-informed decisions of solvent substitution for experimentalists. While some predictions are highly unconventional, this behavior is to be expected from  ML enhanced data-screening applied to a large data set. Note that we have only shown the closest distance suggestions here and experimentalists can obtain a whole list of next-closest suggestions. Most strikingly, the tool succeeds in predicting three green solvent/electrolyte alternatives that were already used in literature, underlining the consistency of the applied methodology.


%To check for redundant information in the predicted parameters, we investigated the correlations between the predicted parameters themselves (see Supplementary Note 2). In this analysis, we find the strongest correlations between the Hansen parameters $\delta_p$ and $\delta_h$ (Pearson coefficient 0.6) as well as the Dielectric Constant $\varepsilon_r$ and the $\delta_d$ (Pearson coefficient 0.69). All other correlation coefficients are below 0.5. Therefore, in accordance with literature\cite{lanComparingCorrelatingSolubility2014, parutaCorrelationSolubilityParameters1962, shiRelationshipHansenSolubility2007,waghorneSolventAcidityBasicity2024},  the chosen solubility parameters are sometimes correlated and clustered, however their complementary information can still help for making better predictions of solubility.

\section{Conclusion and Outlook}

In this work, we present a versatile machine learning pipeline based on transfer learning from the foundational model DimeNet with optional Attention used in conjunction with molecular descriptors. The final layer is a Gaussian Processes model to achieve a data efficient, uncertainty aware, highly robust and accurate framework. We applied this pipeline to the prediction of solubility parameters and the determination of novel, efficient and green solvents. We demonstrate that our pipeline has a good performance not only for the large datasets of the three Hansen solubility parameters and Dielectric constants. It also reaches a good performance on datasets of several hundreds and even less than 50 datapoints for predicting Gutmann Acceptor/Donor numbers as well as Kamlet-Taft solvatochromic parameters. These characteristics uniquely underline the benefits  of transfer learning leveraging pre-trained foundational models. For creating a customizable solvent screening tool, our model is applied to the CompSol database of over 5000 volatile organic compounds, augmenting the available datasets for the given parameters by a factor of 4 for the Hansen parameters, and up to a factor of 100 for other solubility parameters. Since extrapolation is a hard claim, especially for small datasets, we have performed strict 5- to 10-fold validation to avoid presenting unrepresentative optimistic results. In the validation process, our model's performance remained consistently accurate, giving us confidence in the extrapolative power of our results.  Notably, the tool is deployed as an easy-to-use web app for chemists and engineers who are not machine learning experts (see \url{https://kouter.streamlit.app/}). The tool includes customization of distance weighting, uncertainty filtering and
screening filters for eliminating solvents of concern\footnote{the tool is for discovery of new chemicals only and should not be employed for any safety-related decisions}.  On top of that, the codes for the ML process are fully disclosed as well as the obtained, predicted data-sets. In this way, we provide the unique opportunity to the community to re-use our methods and data for their particular application, emphasizing the relevance and impact of this work. An additional benefit of leveraging pre-trained foundational models is that the pipeline is very lightweight - deployment on personal computers is feasible. This further underlines the importance of this work, making it available even to groups without specialized computational infrastructure. In this context, we would like to stress as well that DimeNet is already integrated in the PyTorch library. Since its release however, it has been outperformed by other, more intricate models, notably, MACE and NequIP.
% This tool therefore augments previously available data on these solubility parameters in total by a factor of 4 and for individual parameters of more than one order of magnitude.

In the future, the DimeNet model will be substituted by more recently developed foundational models such as NequIP as the pipeline is fully adaptable. In addition, even more solubility parameters such as the Catalàn parameters or Fawcett Acidity and Basicity will be predicted. Since the pipeline is very adaptable in its target range, it could also be used to predict other functions of chemicals where available data is sparse such as charge transport properties for electronics or reaction pathways. Finally, instead of using a database of known liquid molecules, new molecules that have never been synthesized could be investigated.  The present work should therefore be seen as a proof-of-concept of what this pipeline is able to achieve.


% Continuous integration and improvement by employing more recent foundational models is expected. The employment of the pipeline to the issue of solvent substitution could be a first powerful example of its impact. Specifically, we augment the available data on solubility parameters for relevant liquid chemicals by more than four-fold (measured by numbers of predicted as compared to training data points) and in some parameters by as much as three orders of magnitude.
